# OSDC — 2026-08-25

> 32 advisories analyzed

## GHSA-mw6r-2hvm-4rp2 · qwed-mcp

2026-08-25 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The application used SymPy's `parse_expr()` function to evaluate user-supplied mathematical expressions without sufficient sanitization or a restricted execution environment. This allowed attackers to inject arbitrary Python code, which `parse_expr()` would then execute.

### Impact

An attacker could execute arbitrary Python code on the server, leading to full system compromise, data exfiltration, or denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/qwed_mcp/engines/math_engine.py
+++ b/src/qwed_mcp/engines/math_engine.py
@@ -17,29 +19,19 @@ def verify_math_expression(
     Returns:
         Verification result with verified status and details
     """
     try:
         from sympy import (
-            symbols, sympify, diff, integrate, simplify, solve,
-            Eq, parse_expr, sqrt, sin, cos, exp, log, pi, E
+            symbols, diff, integrate, simplify, solve, Eq,
         )
-        from sympy.parsing.sympy_parser import (
-            parse_expr, standard_transformations,
-            implicit_multiplication_application, convert_xor
-        )
-
         # Common symbol
         x, y, z = symbols('x y z')
-
-        # Transformation for parsing
-        transformations = standard_transformations + (
-            implicit_multiplication_application,
-            convert_xor,
-        )
-
         # Parse expression
         try:
-            expr = parse_expr(
-                expression.replace("^", "**"),
-                local_dict={"x": x, "y": y, "z": z, "pi": pi, "e": E},
-                transformations=transformations
-            )
+            expr = safe_parse_expr(expression.replace("^", "**"))
         except Exception as e:
             return {
                 "verified": False,
                 "message": f"Could not parse expression: {expression}",
                 "error": str(e)
             }
-
         # Parse claimed result
         try:
-            claimed = parse_expr(
-                claimed_result.replace("^", "**"),
-                local_dict={"x": x, "y": y, "z": z, "pi": pi, "e": E},
-                transformations=transformations
-            )
+            claimed = safe_parse_expr(claimed_result.replace("^", "**"))
         except Exception as e:
```

### Fix summary

A new `safe_parser.py` module was introduced, containing `safe_parse_expr()`. This function implements a denylist for dangerous keywords, restricts the global and local dictionaries available during parsing, and enforces a maximum expression length. The `math_engine.py` was updated to use this new safe parser.

## GHSA-w3fx-mc44-mf6j · chainlit

2026-08-25 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The application allowed user-controlled input to be directly executed as a command via `shlex.split` without sufficient validation of the executable itself. While it attempted to restrict executables, an attacker could craft a command string that bypassed these checks, leading to arbitrary command execution.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could achieve remote code execution on the server, gaining full control over the system where Chainlit is running.

### Key diff

```
--- a/backend/chainlit/mcp.py
+++ b/backend/chainlit/mcp.py
@@ -1,10 +1,9 @@
-import shlex
-from typing import Dict, Literal, Optional, Union

+from urllib.parse import unquote, urlparse
 
+import httpx
 from pydantic import BaseModel
 
-from chainlit.config import config
-
 
 class StdioMcpConnection(BaseModel):
     name: str
@@ -29,71 +28,277 @@ class HttpMcpConnection(BaseModel):
 
 McpConnection = Union[StdioMcpConnection, SseMcpConnection, HttpMcpConnection]

-        "x-forwarded-server",

-        # which can reach endpoints the literal request line would not.
-        "x-http-method-override",
-        "x-method-override",
-        "x-original-url",
-        "x-rewrite-url",
-    }
-)

-        base_exec = part.split("/")[-1].split("\\")[-1]

-            break

-    if executable is None or executable_index is None:
-        raise ValueError(
-            f"Only commands in ({', '.join(allowed_exe
+def _path_matches(req_path: str, allowed_path: str) -> bool:
+    """True if req_path equals or is a sub-path of allowed_path.
+
+    Normalises allowed_path to end with '/' before the startswith check so
+    that '/v1' does not accidentally match '/v1-evil'.
+
+    Note: if allowed_path is empty (i.e. the allowlist entry is an origin with
+    no path component, e.g. "https://example.com"), all sub-paths on that host
+    are permitted. Use a path-restricted entry (e.g. "https://example.com/api")
+    to limit access to a sub-tree.
+    """
+    norm = allowed_path.rstrip("/") + "/"
+    return req_path == norm.rstrip("/") or req_path.startswith(norm)
+
+
+def _has_ambiguous_path(raw_path: str) -> bool:
+    """True if raw_path contains constructs that could change meaning in transit.
+
+    We reject rather than normalise. Decoding first would let ``%2f`` create
+    path-segment boundaries that were not in the original URI, manufacturing a
+    passing path from a malicious one, and re-implementing RFC 3986 dot-segment
+    removal would leave us permanently obliged to match httpx byte for byte.
+    None of these constructs belong in a configured MCP endpoint.
+
+    Deliberately operates on the *raw* percent-encoded path rather than
+    ``httpx.URL(url).path``: httpx pre-decodes ``%2e``/``%2f``, which would make
+    the marker check below blind to exactly the sequences it exists to catch.
+    """
+    lowered = raw_path.lower()
+    # %2e -> '.', %2f -> '/', %5c -> '\', %25 -> a second decoding pass
+    # downstream. Backslash is a separator on some origin servers and is left
+    # untouched by httpx.
+    if any(marker in lowered for marker in ("%2e", "%2f", "%5c", "%25")):
+        return True
+    if "\" in raw_path:
+        return True
+    if any(segment in (".", "..") for segment in raw_path.split("/")):
+        return True
+
+    # Non-ASCII characters are rejected outright, before and after a single
+    # decode. Many code points fold to '.', '/' or '\' under the normalisation
+    # some origin servers and proxies apply (U+FF0E, U+2024, U+FF0F, U+2215,
+    # U+2044, ...), which would reopen traversal at the destination. Enumerating
+    # them is a denylist that keeps coming up short, so we require the path to
+    # be ASCII instead — a positive rule, and no real MCP endpoint needs more.
+    if not raw_path.isascii():
+        return True
+    try:
+        decoded = unquote(raw_path, errors="strict")
+    except UnicodeDecodeError:
+        return True
+    return not decoded.isascii()
+
+
+def validate_mcp_url(url: str, allowed_urls: list[str]) -> None:
+    """Validate that a user-provided MCP URL is in the allowlist.
+
+    Raises ValueError if the URL is not permitted.
+    """
+    if not allowed_urls:
+        raise ValueError(
+            f"URL {url!r} is not in the allowed MCP URL list. "
+            "Configure features.mcp.user_servers.allowed_urls in your config."
+        )
+
+    # Parse the request URL through httpx, which is what the MCP transports
+    # dispatch with. Validating anything else risks approving a URL that
+    # differs from the one that actually goes on the wire.
+    try:
+        parsed = httpx.URL(url)
+    except Exception as exc:
+        raise ValueError(
+            f"URL {url!r} is not in the allowed MCP URL list. It could not be parsed."
+        ) from exc
+
+    if parsed.scheme not in ("http", "https"):
+        raise ValueError(
+            f"URL {url!r} is not in the allowed MCP URL list. "
+            "Only http and https URLs are supported."
+        )
+
+    if _has_ambiguous_path(urlparse(url).path):
+        raise ValueError(
+            f"URL {url!r} is not in the allowed MCP URL list. "
+            "It contains ambiguous path components (e.g. '.', '..', or percent-encoded slashes)."
+        )
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the `validate_mcp_command` function, which was responsible for validating and executing commands, and replaces it with `validate_mcp_url`. This new function strictly validates URLs against an allowlist, disallows ambiguous path components, and restricts schemes and headers, preventing arbitrary command execution and SSRF.

## GHSA-6g6r-q6gw-w8fg · PraisonAI

2026-08-25 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.1 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from multiple issues: an unanchored regex in the WebSocket origin validation, allowing malicious subdomains to bypass checks; a lack of API key authentication for critical job endpoints; and insufficient SSRF protection in the web crawling tool, which did not properly validate redirect targets or internal IP addresses.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass origin validation to connect to the WebSocket server from unauthorized domains, execute arbitrary code via the jobs API without authentication, and perform Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) attacks to access internal network resources or sensitive cloud metadata.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/praisonai/praisonai/mcp_server/transports/http_stream.py
+++ b/src/praisonai/praisonai/mcp_server/transports/http_stream.py
-        for allowed in self.allowed_origins:
-            if request_origin == allowed or request_origin.startswith(allowed):
-                return True
-        return False
+        from urllib.parse import urlparse
+        from praisonaiagents.mcp.mcp_security import is_valid_origin
+        allowed_hosts = []
+        for origin in self.allowed_origins:
+            host = urlparse(origin).hostname
+            if host:
+                allowed_hosts.append(host.lower())
+        return is_valid_origin(request_origin, allowed_hosts, allow_missing=False)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a robust origin validation function using `urllib.parse` and `is_valid_origin` to prevent unanchored regex bypasses. It also adds an API key authentication middleware for job endpoints and implements comprehensive SSRF checks in the web crawling tool, including validation of redirect targets and blocking access to private/loopback IP addresses.

## GHSA-8vh3-g2qg-2h2c · nextcloud-mcp-server

2026-08-25 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.1 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The application allowed unauthenticated POST requests to the /webhooks/nextcloud endpoint when the WEBHOOK_SECRET environment variable was not set. This default configuration meant that the user.uid from the webhook payload, which is attacker-controllable, was directly used to delete or re-index vector data in Qdrant without any authentication.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could send crafted webhook requests to the /webhooks/nextcloud endpoint and delete or re-index arbitrary users' vector embeddings, leading to data loss or corruption for other users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nextcloud_mcp_server/vector/webhook_receiver.py
+++ b/nextcloud_mcp_server/vector/webhook_receiver.py
-    if secret:
-        provided = request.headers.get("authorization", "").encode("utf-8")
-        expected = f"Bearer {secret}".encode("utf-8")
-        if not hmac.compare_digest(provided, expected):
-            logger.warning("Webhook rejected: missing or invalid Authorization header")
-            return JSONResponse({"status": "unauthorized"}, status_code=401,)
-    else:
+    if not secret:
+        _warn_missing_secret_once()
+        return JSONResponse({"status": "unavailable", "reason": "webhook authentication not configured"}, status_code=503,)
+
+    provided = request.headers.get("authorization", "").encode("utf-8")
+    expected = f"Bearer {secret}".encode("utf-8")
```

### Fix summary

The patch makes the WEBHOOK_SECRET environment variable mandatory for the /webhooks/nextcloud endpoint to be mounted. If the secret is not set, the route is not exposed, and any direct attempts to call the handler will result in a 503 error. This ensures that all requests to this endpoint are authenticated using a bearer token.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 61 occurrences**

## GHSA-x44h-65qv-cw74 · praisonaiagents

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 8.5 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The original SSRF protection in `_crawl_with_httpx` only validated the initial URL but did not re-validate URLs after redirects. An attacker could provide a URL that initially points to a safe external host but then redirects to an internal or loopback address (e.g., using `127.0.0.1.nip.io` or similar DNS tricks), bypassing the initial check.

### Impact

An attacker could force the application to make requests to internal network resources or cloud metadata endpoints, potentially leading to information disclosure, unauthorized access, or further attacks within the internal network.

### Key diff

```
-                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=True, timeout=30.0) as client:
-                    response = client.get(url)
+                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=False, timeout=30.0) as client:
+                    for _ in range(max_redirects + 1):
+                        if not _is_safe_crawl_url(current):
+                            raise ValueError("Redirect target failed SSRF validation")
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function `_is_safe_crawl_url` which performs SSRF checks, including DNS resolution and IP address validation (checking for loopback, private, link-local, multicast, or unspecified IPs). This function is now called for the initial URL and for every redirect target during the crawling process, ensuring all intermediate and final URLs are safe.

## GHSA-7g3p-92qq-8wvh · praisonaiagents

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 8.2 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The application's job server lacked authentication enforcement on its routes, allowing unauthorized access. Additionally, the web crawling functionality did not adequately validate URLs, making it vulnerable to SSRF. Finally, the MCP server did not limit the number of active sessions, leading to a potential resource exhaustion DoS.

### Impact

An attacker could execute arbitrary jobs, access internal network resources, or cause a denial of service by exhausting server resources. This could lead to data breaches, unauthorized operations, or service unavailability.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/praisonai/praisonai/jobs/server.py
+++ b/src/praisonai/praisonai/jobs/server.py
@@ -123,6 +123,25 @@ def create_app(
             allow_methods=["GET", "POST", "PUT", "DELETE", "OPTIONS"],
             allow_headers=["Authorization", "Content-Type", "Origin", "Accept", "Idempotency-Key"],
         )
+
+    jobs_api_key = os.environ.get("PRAISONAI_JOBS_API_KEY")
+    if jobs_api_key:
+        import hmac
+        from starlette.middleware.base import BaseHTTPMiddleware
+        from starlette.responses import JSONResponse
+
+        class JobsAPIKeyMiddleware(BaseHTTPMiddleware):
+            async def dispatch(self, request, call_next):
+                if request.url.path == "/health":
+                    return await call_next(request)
+                auth = request.headers.get("Authorization", "")
+                header_key = request.headers.get("X-API-Key", "")
+                token = auth[7:] if auth.startswith("Bearer ") else header_key
+                if not token or not hmac.compare_digest(token, jobs_api_key):
+                    return JSONResponse({"error": "Unauthorized"}, status_code=401)
+                return await call_next(request)
+
+        app.add_middleware(JobsAPIKeyMiddleware)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces an API key middleware for the job server to enforce authentication on all routes except '/health'. It also adds robust URL validation to the web crawling tool to prevent SSRF by disallowing private, loopback, and other unsafe IP addresses. Furthermore, a maximum session limit and a session cleanup mechanism were added to the MCP server to mitigate DoS attacks.

## GHSA-hxmv-c4g6-5fqc · PraisonAI

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 7.8 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application's web crawling functionality did not adequately validate URLs, allowing it to make requests to internal network resources or local files. Specifically, the previous implementation of `_crawl_with_httpx` and `web_crawl` lacked robust checks for private IP addresses or loopback addresses, and also did not properly handle redirects to potentially malicious internal URLs.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious URL to force the server to make requests to internal services, access sensitive internal resources, or potentially read local files, leading to information disclosure or further network compromise.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/praisonai-agents/praisonaiagents/tools/web_crawl_tools.py
+++ b/src/praisonai-agents/praisonaiagents/tools/web_crawl_tools.py
@@ -130,17 +130,64 @@ async def _crawl_async():
     except RuntimeError:
         return asyncio.run(_crawl_async())
 
+def _is_safe_crawl_url(url: str) -> bool:
+    """Return True when a URL passes SSRF checks for crawling."""
+    import urllib.parse
+    import socket
+    import ipaddress
+
+    try:
+        parsed = urllib.parse.urlparse(url)
+        if parsed.scheme not in ("http", "https"):
+            return False
+        hostname = parsed.hostname
+        if not hostname:
+            return False
+        if os.environ.get("ALLOW_LOCAL_CRAWL") == "true":
+            return True
+        for info in socket.getaddrinfo(hostname, None):
+            ip = ipaddress.ip_address(info[4][0])
+            if (
+                ip.is_loopback
+                or ip.is_private
+                or ip.is_link_local
+                or ip.is_multicast
+                or ip.is_unspecified
+            ):
+                return False
+        return True
+    except (socket.gaierror, ValueError, OSError):
+        return False
+
+
 def _crawl_with_httpx(urls: List[str]) -> List[Dict[str, Any]]:
     """Crawl URLs using basic HTTP fetch with httpx or urllib."""
+    import urllib.parse
     results = []
-    
+    redirect_statuses = {301, 302, 303, 307, 308}
+    max_redirects = 5
+
     for url in urls:
         try:
             # Try httpx first
             try:
                 import httpx
-                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=True, timeout=30.0) as client:
-                    response = client.get(url)
+                current = url
+                response = None
+                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=False, timeout=30.0) as client:
+                    for _ in range(max_redirects + 1):
+                        if not _is_safe_crawl_url(current):
+                            raise ValueError("Redirect target failed SSRF validation")
+                        response = client.get(current)
+                        if response.status_code not in redirect_statuses:
+                            break
+                        location = response.headers.get("Location")
+                        if not location:
+                            break
+                        current = urllib.parse.urljoin(current, location)
+                    else:
+                        raise ValueError("Too many redirects")
+                if response is not None:
                     response.raise_for_status()
                     content = response.text
             except ImportError:
@@ -210,37 +257,12 @@ def web_crawl(
         raw_url_list = [urls] if single_url else urls
 
     # Validate URLs to prevent SSRF and Local File Read
-    import urllib.parse
-    import socket
-    import ipaddress
-    
     url_list = []
     for u in raw_url_list:
-        try:
-            parsed = urllib.parse.urlparse(u)
-            if parsed.scheme not in ('http', 'https'):
-                logger.warning(f"Rejected non-http/https URL: {u}")
-                continue
-            hostname = parsed.hostname
-            if not hostname
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function `_is_safe_crawl_url` to perform comprehensive SSRF checks, including validating URL schemes, hostnames, and ensuring IP addresses are not loopback, private, link-local, multicast, or unspecified. It also modifies the `_crawl_with_httpx` function to apply these safety checks to redirect targets and limits the number of redirects to prevent infinite loops or excessive resource consumption. Additionally, API key authentication is added to the jobs server, and session limits are introduced for the MCP server to mitigate DoS.

## GHSA-5r34-2g38-6569 · praisonaiagents

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The `web_crawl` tool in `praisonaiagents` did not properly validate URLs when following redirects. While initial URLs were checked for SSRF, subsequent redirect locations were not, allowing an attacker to bypass the initial validation and redirect the application to internal network resources or other restricted locations.

### Impact

An attacker could force the application to make requests to arbitrary internal network resources, potentially accessing sensitive data, interacting with internal services, or scanning internal networks.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/praisonai-agents/praisonaiagents/tools/web_crawl_tools.py
+++ b/src/praisonai-agents/praisonaiagents/tools/web_crawl_tools.py
@@ -130,17 +130,64 @@ async def _crawl_async():
     except RuntimeError:
         return asyncio.run(_crawl_async())
 
+def _is_safe_crawl_url(url: str) -> bool:
+    """Return True when a URL passes SSRF checks for crawling."""
+    import urllib.parse
+    import socket
+    import ipaddress
+
+    try:
+        parsed = urllib.parse.urlparse(url)
+        if parsed.scheme not in ("http", "https"):
+            return False
+        hostname = parsed.hostname
+        if not hostname:
+            return False
+        if os.environ.get("ALLOW_LOCAL_CRAWL") == "true":
+            return True
+        for info in socket.getaddrinfo(hostname, None):
+            ip = ipaddress.ip_address(info[4][0])
+            if (
+                ip.is_loopback
+                or ip.is_private
+                or ip.is_link_local
+                or ip.is_multicast
+                or ip.is_unspecified
+            ):
+                return False
+        return True
+    except (socket.gaierror, ValueError, OSError):
+        return False
+
+
 def _crawl_with_httpx(urls: List[str]) -> List[Dict[str, Any]]:
     """Crawl URLs using basic HTTP fetch with httpx or urllib."""
+    import urllib.parse
     results = []
-    
+    redirect_statuses = {301, 302, 303, 307, 308}
+    max_redirects = 5
+
     for url in urls:
         try:
             # Try httpx first
             try:
                 import httpx
-                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=True, timeout=30.0) as client:
-                    response = client.get(url)
+                current = url
+                response = None
+                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=False, timeout=30.0) as client:
+                    for _ in range(max_redirects + 1):
+                        if not _is_safe_crawl_url(current):
+                            raise ValueError("Redirect target failed SSRF validation")
+                        response = client.get(current)
+                        if response.status_code not in redirect_statuses:
+                            break
+                        location = response.headers.get("Location")

+                            break
+                        current = urllib.parse.urljoin(current, location)
+                    else:
+                        raise ValueError("Too many redirects")

                     response.raise_for_status()
                     content = response.text

```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function `_is_safe_crawl_url` to validate URLs, including redirect targets, against SSRF checks. It modifies the `_crawl_with_httpx` function to explicitly disable automatic redirect following and manually handle redirects, applying the `_is_safe_crawl_url` check to each redirect location before making the request.

## GHSA-cv84-9p8j-fj68 · icalendar

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The original `__eq__` method for `Component` objects performed a nested loop comparison of subcomponents. For each subcomponent in `self.subcomponents`, it iterated through `other.subcomponents` to find a match. If both lists of subcomponents were large and contained many non-matching elements, this could lead to a quadratic time complexity (O(n*m)) in the worst case, where n and m are the number of subcomponents in the two lists.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious iCalendar component with a large number of subcomponents, which, when compared for equality with another component, would consume excessive CPU resources. This could lead to a denial of service (DoS) by making the application unresponsive or very slow when processing such crafted inputs.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/icalendar/cal/component.py
+++ b/src/icalendar/cal/component.py
@@ -627,29 +627,39 @@ def __repr__(self):
         return "".join(out)
 
     def __eq__(self, other):
-        if len(self.subcomponents) != len(other.subcomponents):
-            return False
-        # ... (omitted original nested loop comparison)
-        for subcomponent in self.subcomponents:
-            if subcomponent not in other.subcomponents:
-                return False
-        return True
+        if not isinstance(other, Component):
+            return NotImplemented
+
+        stack = [[self, other, None, 0, 0]]
+        child_result = None
+        while stack:
+            frame = stack[-1]
+            a, b, unmatched, i, j = frame
+            if unmatched is None:
+                if len(a.subcomponents) != len(b.subcomponents) or not (
+                    CaselessDict.__eq__(a, b)
+                ):
+                    stack.pop()
+                    child_result = False
+                    continue
+                unmatched = frame[2] = list(b.subcomponents)
+            elif child_result is not None:
+                if child_result:
+                    del unmatched[j]
+                    i = frame[3] = i + 1
+                    j = frame[4] = 0
+                else:
+                    j = frame[4] = j + 1
+                child_result = None
+            if i >= len(a.subcomponents):
+                stack.pop()
+                child_result = True
+            elif j >= len(unmatched):
+                stack.pop()
+                child_result = False
+            else:
+                stack.append([a.subcomponents[i], unmatched[j], None, 0, 0])
+        return child_result
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors the `__eq__` method to use an iterative, stack-based approach for comparing subcomponents. Instead of repeated linear searches, it maintains a list of unmatched subcomponents from 'other' and removes them as matches are found, effectively reducing the complexity of the comparison. Additionally, the `to_jcal` and `from_jcal` methods were refactored to use iterative approaches instead of recursion to prevent potential stack overflow issues with deeply nested components.

## GHSA-g7gc-gmgp-wgqg · eml_parser

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→INFINITE_LOOP`

### Root cause

The `eml_parser` library used a regular expression-based approach (`noparenthesis_regex.sub`) in a loop to remove nested parentheses from email headers. For deeply nested or pathological input, this recursive-descent-like processing could lead to excessive CPU consumption and potentially a `RecursionError` in the underlying Python standard library's email parsing, causing a Denial of Service.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious email with deeply nested parentheses in headers (e.g., 'Received' or 'To') to cause the `eml_parser` to consume excessive CPU resources, leading to a Denial of Service for applications processing such emails.

### Key diff

```
--- a/eml_parser/routing.py
+++ b/eml_parser/routing.py
@@ -10,21 +10,19 @@
 
 
 def noparenthesis(line: str) -> str:
-    line_ = line
-    while True:
-        lline = line_
-        line_ = eml_parser.regexes.noparenthesis_regex.sub('', line_)
-        if lline == line_:
-            break
-    return line_
+    fragments: list[list[str]] = [[]]
+    for ch in line:
+        if ch == '(': fragments.append([ch])
+        else: fragments[-1].append(ch)
+        if ch == ')' and len(fragments) > 1: fragments.pop()
+    return ''.join(ch for w in fragments for ch in w)
```

### Fix summary

The patch replaces the problematic regex-based `noparenthesis` function with an iterative, stack-like approach that correctly handles and removes nested parentheses without excessive recursion. It also adds a `try-except RecursionError` block for specific headers to gracefully handle pathological inputs by falling back to a simpler email address extraction if the standard library parser fails.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 15 occurrences**
> Languages: JavaScript, Python, Java, Erlang, RUST, PHP
> → GHSA-4rc3-7j7w-m548
> → GHSA-grgv-6hw6-v9g4

## GHSA-hvfh-5mj3-5f3j · chainlit

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 7.2 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The Chainlit application allowed user-supplied URLs for its MCP SSE and streamable-http transports without sufficient validation. This permitted requests to arbitrary internal network resources, bypassing intended access controls.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could make the Chainlit server perform requests to internal network services, potentially accessing sensitive information, interacting with internal APIs, or port scanning the internal network.

### Key diff

```
--- a/backend/chainlit/mcp.py
+++ b/backend/chainlit/mcp.py
@@ -269,3 +269,57 @@
         raise ValueError(
             f"Only commands in ({', '.join(allowed_exe
 
+def validate_mcp_url(url: str, allowed_urls: list[str]) -> None:
+    """Validate that a user-provided MCP URL is in the allowlist.
+
+    Raises ValueError if the URL is not permitted.
+    """
+    if not allowed_urls:
+        raise ValueError(
+            f"URL {url!r} is not in the allowed MCP URL list. "
+            "Configure features.mcp.user_servers.allowed_urls in your config."
+        )
+
+    # Parse the request URL through httpx, which is what the MCP transports
+    # dispatch with. Validating anything else risks approving a URL that
+    # differs from the one that actually goes on the wire.
+    try:
+        parsed = httpx.URL(url)
+    except Exception as exc:
+        raise ValueError(
+            f"URL {url!r} is not in the allowed MCP URL list. It could not be parsed."
+        ) from exc
+
+    if parsed.scheme not in ("http", "https"):
+        raise ValueError(
+            f"URL {url!r} is not in the allowed MCP URL list. "
+            "Only http and https URLs are supported."
+        )
+
+    if _has_ambiguous_path(urlparse(url).path):
+        raise ValueError(
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a robust URL validation mechanism for MCP connections. It ensures that URLs adhere to an explicit allowlist, use only 'http' or 'https' schemes, and rejects URLs with ambiguous or potentially malicious path components (e.g., percent-encoded slashes, dot-segments, non-ASCII characters) to prevent path traversal and bypasses.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 117 occurrences**

## GHSA-xc9g-j69q-37xw · sublinear-time-solver

2026-08-25 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.1 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_WRITE`

### Root cause

The application allowed an attacker-controlled file_path argument in the `saveVectorToFile` and `loadVectorFromFile` functions to be directly used with `path.resolve()` and `fs.writeFileSync()`/`fs.readFileSync()`. This meant that an attacker could specify arbitrary file paths, leading to writing or reading files outside the intended directory.

### Impact

An attacker could write arbitrary files to any location on the server filesystem that the MCP process had write permissions to, potentially leading to remote code execution, denial of service, or data corruption. They could also read arbitrary files, leading to sensitive information disclosure.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/mcp/server.ts
+++ b/src/mcp/server.ts
@@ -1262,10 +1285,10 @@ export class SublinearSolverMCPServer {
         throw new Error(`Unsupported format: ${fileFormat}`);
     }
 
-    fs.writeFileSync(absolutePath, content, 'utf8');
+    const absolutePath = safeWriteVector(filePath, content);
     console.log(`💾 Saved vector of size ${vector.length} to ${absolutePath} (${fileFormat} format)`);
+    return absolutePath;
   }
 
   private getFileFormat(filePath: string, explicitFormat?: string): string {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `safe-path.ts` module with functions like `safeWriteVector` and `safeReadVector`. These functions enforce that file paths are treated as basenames only, preventing path traversal by resolving them within a dedicated, configurable vector directory (`DEFAULT_VECTOR_DIR`). Input validation was also added to the `file_path` argument to ensure it does not contain path separators or special directory names.

## GHSA-wj6g-v78p-6fx3 · PraisonAI

2026-08-25 · Python · **MODERATE** 6.9 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The PraisonAI MCP HTTP Stream transport had an insufficient origin validation mechanism, allowing crafted Origin headers to bypass security checks. Additionally, the web crawling functionality lacked robust SSRF protection, especially concerning redirects, enabling access to internal resources.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass origin validation to execute unauthenticated tools on the local MCP server via browser-mediated requests. Furthermore, the SSRF vulnerability in the web crawling tool could be exploited to access internal network resources or sensitive cloud metadata.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/praisonai/praisonai/mcp_server/transports/http_stream.py
+++ b/src/praisonai/praisonai/mcp_server/transports/http_stream.py
@@ -142,13 +144,17 @@ def _validate_origin(self, request_origin: Optional[str]) -> bool:
         if self.allowed_origins is None:
             # No allowed origins configured - reject all Origin headers
             return False
-        
-        # Check if origin matches any allowed origin
-        for allowed in self.allowed_origins:
-            if request_origin == allowed or request_origin.startswith(allowed):
-                return True
-        
-        return False
+
+        from urllib.parse import urlparse
+        from praisonaiagents.mcp.mcp_security import is_valid_origin
+
+        allowed_hosts = []
+        for origin in self.allowed_origins:
+            host = urlparse(origin).hostname
+            if host:
+                allowed_hosts.append(host.lower())
+
+        return is_valid_origin(request_origin, allowed_hosts, allow_missing=False)
```

```diff
--- a/src/praisonai-agents/praisonaiagents/tools/web_crawl_tools.py
+++ b/src/praisonai-agents/praisonaiagents/tools/web_crawl_tools.py
@@ -130,17 +130,64 @@ async def _crawl_async():
     except RuntimeError:
         return asyncio.run(_crawl_async())
 
+def _is_safe_crawl_url(url: str) -> bool:
+    """Return True when a URL passes SSRF checks for crawling."""
+    import urllib.parse
+    import socket
+    import ipaddress
+
+    try:
+        parsed = urllib.parse.urlparse(url)
+        if parsed.scheme not in ("http", "https"):
+            return False
+        hostname = parsed.hostname
+        if not hostname:
+            return False
+        if os.environ.get("ALLOW_LOCAL_CRAWL") == "true":
+            return True
+        for info in socket.getaddrinfo(hostname, None):
+            ip = ipaddress.ip_address(info[4][0])
+            if (
+                ip.is_loopback
+                or ip.is_private
+                or ip.is_link_local
+                or ip.is_multicast
+                or ip.is_unspecified
+            ):
+                return False
+        return True
+    except (socket.gaierror, ValueError, OSError):
+        return False
+
+
 def _crawl_with_httpx(urls: List[str]) -> List[Dict[str, Any]]:
     """Crawl URLs using basic HTTP fetch with httpx or urllib."""
+    import urllib.parse
     results = []
-    
+    redirect_statuses = {301, 302, 303, 307, 308}
+    max_redirects = 5
+
     for url in urls:
         try:
             # Try httpx first
             try:
                 import httpx
-                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=True, timeout=30.0) as client:
-                    response = client.get(url)
+                current = url
+                response = None
+                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=False, timeout=30.0) as client:
+                    for _ in range(max_redirects + 1):
+                        if not _is_safe_crawl_url(current):
+                            raise ValueError("Redirect target failed SSRF validation")
+                        response = client.get(current)
+                        if response.status_code not in redirect_statuses:
+                            break
+                        location = response.headers.get("Location")
+                        if not location:
+                            break
+                        current = urllib.parse.urljoin(current, location)
+                    else:
+                        raise ValueError("Too many redirects")
+                if response is not None:
                     response.raise_for_status()
                     content = response.text
             except ImportError:
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a more robust origin validation using `urllib.parse` and a new `is_valid_origin` helper. It also adds an API key authentication middleware for the jobs server. For the web crawling tool, a new `_is_safe_crawl_url` function is implemented to validate URLs and redirect targets against SSRF, preventing access to local, private, or loopback IPs.

## GHSA-wv94-5qcp-6m36 · PraisonAI

2026-08-25 · Python · **MODERATE** 6.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The PraisonAI MCP HTTP server allowed unauthenticated clients to create an unbounded number of sessions, leading to memory exhaustion. Additionally, the web crawling functionality lacked robust SSRF protection, allowing an attacker to potentially access internal resources or conduct port scanning. The jobs API also lacked authentication by default.

### Impact

An attacker could exhaust server memory, causing a denial of service. They could also potentially access internal network resources or cloud metadata services via the web crawling tool, and access or manipulate job-related functionalities without proper authorization.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/praisonai/praisonai/mcp_server/transports/http_stream.py
+++ b/src/praisonai/praisonai/mcp_server/transports/http_stream.py
@@ -217,6 +224,11 @@ async def mcp_post(request: Request) -> Response:
             
             # Check if this is an initialize request
             if body.get("method") == "initialize":
+                if len(self._sessions) >= self.max_sessions:
+                    return JSONResponse(
+                        {"jsonrpc": "2.0", "id": body.get("id"), "error": {"code": -32000, "message": "Too many active sessions"}},
+                        status_code=503,
+                    )
                 # Create new session
                 new_session_id = str(uuid.uuid4())
                 self._sessions[new_session_id] = {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a maximum session limit for the MCP server, configurable via an environment variable, and adds a session cleanup mechanism. It also implements stricter SSRF checks for the web crawling tool, including redirect validation. Furthermore, an optional API key authentication middleware was added for the jobs server.

## GHSA-6ccx-9c9f-327w · grpc

2026-08-25 · Erlang · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The gRPC Erlang package used the :zlib.gunzip function without any limits on the size of the decompressed output. An attacker could send a specially crafted, small compressed message that, when decompressed, would expand to an extremely large size, consuming excessive memory.

### Impact

An attacker could trigger a denial of service by causing the server to exhaust its memory resources, leading to application crashes or unresponsiveness.

### Key diff

```
--- a/grpc_core/lib/grpc/compressor/gzip.ex
+++ b/grpc_core/lib/grpc/compressor/gzip.ex
@@ -10,5 +18,34 @@
   end
 
   def decompress(data) do
-    :zlib.gunzip(data)
+    max_size = Application.get_env(:grpc, :max_decompressed_message_length, @default_max_decompressed_size)
+    z = :zlib.open()
+    :ok = :zlib.inflateInit(z, 31)
+    try do
+      chunks = inflate_chunks(z, data, max_size, 0, [])
+      :zlib.inflateEnd(z)
+      IO.iodata_to_binary(chunks)
+    after
+      :zlib.close(z)
+    end
   end
+
+  defp inflate_chunks(_z, <<>>, _max_size, _acc_size, acc), do: acc
+
+  defp inflate_chunks(z, data, max_size, acc_size, acc) do
+    {chunk, rest} = split_chunk(data)
+    output = :zlib.inflate(z, chunk)
+    new_size = acc_size + IO.iodata_length(output)
+
+    if new_size > max_size do
+      raise GRPC.RPCError,
+        status: :resource_exhausted,
+        message: "Decompressed message exceeds limit of #{max_size} bytes"
+    end
+
+    inflate_chunks(z, rest, max_size, new_size, [acc, output])
+  end
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a maximum decompressed message length (defaulting to 4 MB) and processes the decompression in chunks. It checks the accumulated decompressed size against the limit and raises an error if the limit is exceeded, preventing unbounded memory allocation.

## GHSA-777c-2fxx-qr28 · ash_authentication

2026-08-25 · Erlang · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The AshAuthentication library's OAuth2/OIDC strategy allowed for account takeover if an attacker could register with an OAuth2/OIDC provider using a victim's email address. The system would match users based solely on the email claim from the untrusted OAuth2/OIDC provider, without sufficient verification of email ownership or a confirmation step.

### Impact

An attacker could link their OAuth2/OIDC account to an existing user's account in the application, effectively taking over the victim's account if the victim's email was used by the attacker to register with the OAuth2/OIDC provider.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/ash_authentication/strategies/oauth2.ex
+++ b/ash_authentication/strategies/oauth2.ex
@@ -239,6 +239,7 @@ defmodule AshAuthentication.Strategy.OAuth2 do
     identity_resource: false,
     name: nil,
     nonce: false,
+    on_untrusted_email_match: :reject,
     prevent_hijacking?: true,
     openid_configuration_uri: nil,
     openid_configuration: nil,
@@ -255,6 +256,7 @@ defmodule AshAuthentication.Strategy.OAuth2 do
     strategy_module: __MODULE__,
     team_id: nil,
     token_url: nil,
+    trust_email_verified?: false,
     trusted_audiences: nil,
     user_url: nil,
     code_verifier: false,
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces new configuration options: `on_untrusted_email_match` and `trust_email_verified?`. By default, `on_untrusted_email_match` is set to `:reject`, preventing automatic linking based on untrusted emails. If set to `:confirm`, it now requires a `confirmation` add-on to be configured, ensuring a verification step for email ownership before linking accounts.

## GHSA-8hjw-25cg-g52h · praisonaiagents

2026-08-25 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application's web crawling functionality, intended to prevent Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) by validating initial URLs, failed to re-validate URLs after HTTP redirects. This allowed an attacker to provide an initial safe URL that would then redirect to an unsafe internal or private network resource, bypassing the initial security check.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass the existing SSRF protection to access internal network resources, potentially leading to information disclosure, unauthorized access to internal services, or further attacks within the private network.

### Key diff

```
-                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=True, timeout=30.0) as client:
-                    response = client.get(url)
+                with httpx.Client(follow_redirects=False, timeout=30.0) as client:
+                    for _ in range(max_redirects + 1):
+                        if not _is_safe_crawl_url(current):
+                            raise ValueError("Redirect target failed SSRF validation")
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `_crawl_with_httpx` function to explicitly disable automatic redirects and manually follow them. Each redirect target URL is now re-validated using a new `_is_safe_crawl_url` function to ensure it does not point to loopback, private, link-local, multicast, or unspecified IP addresses, effectively preventing SSRF via redirects.

## GHSA-grp7-v8xh-rj7h · grpc

2026-08-25 · Erlang · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `DESERIALIZATION→RCE`

### Root cause

The `GRPC.Codec.Erlpack` module used `:erlang.binary_to_term/1` to decode untrusted gRPC payloads. This function, without the `:safe` option and additional checks, could deserialize arbitrary Erlang terms, including functions, PIDs, ports, or references. An attacker could craft a malicious payload that, when deserialized, would lead to remote code execution or atom table exhaustion.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve remote code execution on the server by sending a specially crafted gRPC payload. Additionally, they could cause a denial of service by exhausting the atom table.

### Key diff

```
--- a/grpc_core/lib/grpc/codec/erlpack.ex
+++ b/grpc_core/lib/grpc/codec/erlpack.ex
@@ -10,6 +27,38 @@ defmodule GRPC.Codec.Erlpack do
   end
 
   def decode(binary, _module) do
-    :erlang.binary_to_term(binary)
+    term = :erlang.binary_to_term(binary, [:safe])
+    ensure_safe_term!(term)
+    term
   end
+
+  defp ensure_safe_term!(term)
+       when is_function(term) or is_pid(term) or is_port(term) or is_reference(term) do
+    raise ArgumentError,
+          "refusing to decode unsafe erlpack payload containing a #{term_type(term)}"
+  end
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `GRPC.Codec.Erlpack.decode/2` function to use `:erlang.binary_to_term/2` with the `:safe` option. It also adds a new `ensure_safe_term!/1` function that recursively traverses the decoded term and raises an `ArgumentError` if it encounters any functions, PIDs, ports, or references, which are considered unsafe in gRPC payloads.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 23 occurrences**
> Languages: Java, Python, PHP, JavaScript, Ruby, Erlang

## GHSA-mwr4-5g34-j5cq · grpc

2026-08-25 · Erlang · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `TYPE_CONFUSION→BYPASS`

### Root cause

The gRPC Erlang package's transcoding logic incorrectly merged request parameters. When combining path bindings with query string or request body parameters, the `Map.merge` function was used in an order that allowed query or body parameters to overwrite path bindings, rather than path bindings taking precedence.

### Impact

An attacker could manipulate request parameters by providing values in the query string or request body that would override the intended values from the URL path bindings. This could lead to unexpected behavior or bypass security checks if the path bindings were meant to enforce specific values.

### Key diff

```
-    request = Map.merge(path_bindings, query)
+    request = Map.merge(query, path_bindings)

-    request = Map.merge(path_bindings, body_request)
+    request = Map.merge(body_request, path_bindings)

-    request = Enum.reduce([query, body_request], path_bindings, &Map.merge(&2, &1))
+    request = Enum.reduce([query, body_request], path_bindings, &Map.merge(&1, &2))
```

### Fix summary

The patch reverses the order of arguments in `Map.merge` operations and `Enum.reduce` for request parameter processing. This ensures that path bindings are merged last, giving them precedence over parameters provided in the query string or request body, thus preventing them from being overridden.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 6 occurrences**

> → GHSA-g8f2-4f4f-5jqw
> → GHSA-429q-fhh4-r6hj
> → GHSA-6j2x-vhqr-qr7q

## GHSA-q8gf-9rvj-gmgj · grpc

2026-08-25 · Erlang · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The `read_full_body/3` function in the gRPC Erlang package accumulated the request body without any size limit. An attacker could send an arbitrarily large request body, causing the server to consume excessive memory and potentially crash or become unresponsive.

### Impact

An attacker could trigger a denial of service by sending a large request, leading to resource exhaustion (memory) on the server and making it unavailable to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/grpc_server/lib/grpc/server/adapters/cowboy/handler.ex
+++ b/grpc_server/lib/grpc/server/adapters/cowboy/handler.ex
@@ -617,12 +630,27 @@ defmodule GRPC.Server.Adapters.Cowboy.Handler do
     end
   end
 
-  defp read_full_body(req, body, timer) do
+  defp read_full_body(req, body, timer, max_bytes) do
     result = :cowboy_req.read_body(req, timeout_left_opt(timer))
 
     case result do
-      {:ok, data, req} -> {:ok, body <> data, req}
-      {:more, data, req} -> read_full_body(req, body <> data, timer)
+      {:ok, data, req} ->
+        total = body <> data
+
+        if byte_size(total) > max_bytes do
+          throw({:body_too_large, byte_size(total)})
+        else
+          {:ok, total, req}
+        end
+
+      {:more, data, req} ->
+        total = body <> data
+
+        if byte_size(total) > max_bytes do
+          throw({:body_too_large, byte_size(total)})
+        else
+          read_full_body(req, total, timer, max_bytes)
+        end
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `max_body_size` option, defaulting to 4 MB, to limit the size of incoming request bodies. The `read_full_body` function now checks the accumulated body size against this limit and throws an error if it's exceeded, preventing unbounded memory consumption.
